Until 2026-09-30 the tier seeded a fresh build only, so the walkthrough Save had refused for a blank tier came back from Edit Client still priced at the top of the scale. Two controls that both claimed to decide it would be the two-copies-drift bug again.

> **⚠⚠ The estimate will not commit a price until both contract questions are answered (new 2026-09-22).** Build Estimate refuses to **save**, **submit for approval** or **approve** an estate quote while either the tier or the matter type is blank on the client. It does **not** refuse to price one: the rooms still score, the total still renders, and a notice above the fee panel says it is not a quote yet and why. That split is the whole design — the tier is a question the concierge asks the attorney *while looking at what each answer costs*, so gating the arithmetic would take away the one thing that makes it answerable. A refusal names the unanswered question, says to answer it on **Edit Client**, and says the walkthrough on screen is kept while you do.

> **⚠⚠ A blank tier was never a neutral answer — it was the most expensive one, taken silently.** Measured on the real engine before the gate was built, on a 3,500 sqft probate estate with six rooms scored: an unanswered tier priced at **$17,700** against **$11,600** at *None*. **$6,100 and 51 hours** of documentation work, on a job where nobody had said we were doing any — and the agreement then promised the §733.604 inventory and the 60-day appraisal coordination **in writing**, on a contract the personal representative signs. (The appraisal half has narrowed since: the 60-day promise is written only on *Inventory + appraisals*, or for the appraisers an estimate lists — above.) The pricing fallback is deliberately unchanged: an unanswered job still *prices* at the top, which is what the notice warns about. What changed is that it can no longer be committed without somebody saying so.

> **⚠ And the record was answering the question for you.** Until 2026-09-22 intake saved `docScope: full` beside a **blank** tier, and the tier reader migrates a missing answer back through the scope — so a job saved this morning with the question deliberately left blank read back as *Inventory with values*, indistinguishable from a job recorded before the field existed. Measured on the real intake form: every job the app had ever created came back answered, so the gate could not have fired on any of them. The mirror is blank when the tier is blank now. **The migration still rescues a genuinely old job** — one carrying `docScope` and no tier still reads *Inventory with values* — so nothing priced before today is blocked.

> **Two questions, two tests, and they stay separate.** The matter type is asked of every decedent job; the tier is asked of every service that prices a documentation step. Those select the same three services today and they are not the same question, so each has its own predicate — a living-client job is asked neither, and is never gated. The refusal and the on-screen notice are two renderings of **one** list: the refusal is past tense (*you pressed Save and here is why it stopped*), the notice is present tense (*this is not a quote yet*), and only the notice names what a blank tier costs, because only the notice is read while there is still a decision to make.

### How the estate is being administered — probate, trust, both or neither

New 2026-09-21, in the estate block, **required on all three decedent services and deliberately without a default**. It records which *instrument* the work feeds:

| Answer | What it means |
|---|---|
| **Probate** | Administered through the court. The §733.604 schedule is the instrument, and a personal representative adopts it. |
| **Trust** | A successor trustee administers it under Chapter 736. There is no court filing for a schedule to be adopted into; our record supports the trustee's accounting. |
| **Both** | A pour-over will — some assets probate *into* the trust. Genuinely both, which is why this is not a yes/no flag. |
| **Neither** | Family distribution: no court, no trust. |

> **⚠ It is not derivable from anything else the app holds, which is why it is a question rather than a calculation.** The *service type* is what Havellin was hired to do — *Probate Estate Settlement* means a probate case was open at intake and says nothing about a decedent whose assets pass under a revocable trust. The *representative's role* is who signs, and offers *Trustee*, but a personal representative is frequently the successor trustee as well. The *asset track* is per item and now takes its *default* **from** this answer, which is the wrong way round to derive it from. None of the three answers this.

> **⚠ And it has no default, unlike the inventory question beside it.** Doing the most is a defensible assumption about scope; there is none about probate versus trust, and guessing probate is exactly what puts a court schedule in front of a successor trustee. Anthony's own reading of the book is that *"most homes will be in trust … the probate cases will be more rare if we stick to our upmarket business plan"* — so the guess would be wrong more often than right. **A job recorded before 2026-09-21 carries no answer**, and every reader is written to treat that as unanswered rather than as probate. Edit Client carries the field on every estate service.

> **⚠ The valuation date is NOT one of the things that differs — do not add a trust valuation date.** At death, tangible personal property is valued at fair market value *as of the date of death*, and that one number does three jobs: it sets the beneficiaries' stepped-up basis under IRC §1014, it is the Form 706 figure if one is filed, and it is the **carrying value** a §736.08135 trust accounting opens with. The §1014 step-up reaches revocable trust assets, so a successor trustee needs the same number a personal representative does. What differs is the instrument, the citation, who signs, and that our schedule *supports* a trustee's accounting rather than being one.

> **Where the answer actually goes — eight readers now (six until 2026-10-05).** (1) The **Court Inventory** prints a block on its face on a matter recorded *Trust* or *Neither*, and never finalises or signs it (§10a). (2) The **client’s Drive workbook** drops *Letters Issued*, the *§733.604 Inventory Deadline* and the *Exempt §732.402* count from its Summary sheet, and so does the on-screen summary above it — the screen and the spreadsheet must never disagree about what a matter is. (3) The **Trust Schedule** is the instrument a trust matter actually gets (§10a). (4) The per-item **Asset Track default** follows it. (5) The **estate agreement** stops asserting a probate proceeding: §5.2 since 2026-09-21, and since 2026-10-03 (P19) the whole form — the party, the paper that proves the representative's authority, the case number and court, counsel, the approver and the co-signers (§8). (6) **Exhibit A**, the client estimate, asks for the matter's own paper and routes the proceeds to whoever holds the property (since 2026-10-03, §7). (7) The **service's name on every client document**: a Probate or Contested Probate service on a matter recorded *Trust* or *Neither* is named without "Probate" (since 2026-10-05). (8) The **Probate flag** on Client Intake, Edit Client and Build Estimate (since 2026-10-05). Both are in the next section. An unanswered matter type still changes nothing anywhere.

> **⚠⚠ THE ASSET TRACK DEFAULT NOW FOLLOWS THIS ANSWER, AND UNTIL 2026-09-21 IT COULD NOT (§10a).** It defaulted every unset item to *Probate*, and this manual told you to set it by hand on a trust matter. That instruction is **withdrawn** — the default is *Trust* on a matter recorded *Trust*. **⚠ Only an explicit *Trust* moves it.** *Both* keeps *Probate*, deliberately: a pour-over will's whole function is that anything not already titled into the trust pours in *through* probate, so an untracked item there is probate property until somebody says otherwise. *Neither* and an unanswered matter keep *Probate* too, so **no job recorded before today moves an inch**. A track you set by hand always wins over the default, in both directions.

### A Probate service on a matter with no court (new 2026-10-05, P20)

The service is what Havellin was hired to do, and it prices the job; the matter is how the estate is administered. The two can disagree: a **Probate Estate Settlement** or **Contested Probate Estate Settlement** service on a matter recorded *Trust* or *Neither* has no court. *Both* and an unanswered matter are on the probate track, so nothing here applies to them. Anthony's answer (Q26, *A*): flag it and suggest Estate Settlement, never switch it for you, because pricing is his call; and on such a matter no document claims a probate.

- **The flag, on Probate only.** An amber box: *"No court on this matter: it is recorded as a trust administration* (on *Neither*: *a family distribution, with no trust*)*. The Probate Estate Settlement service prices court work that will not happen. Switch the service to Estate Settlement before the estimate is approved; it reprices the estimate."* It appears on **Client Intake** under *How is this estate being administered?* as either the service or the matter changes; on **✎ Edit Client** under the same question, on open and as you change either, and again in the notice after **Save Changes**; and on **Build Estimate** in the Estimate Summary, under the total — in field mode, which hides the summary, beside the service picker instead (since 2026-10-06, P22). While the estimate is out for manager approval it names the route instead: the estimate opens for editing again once a manager approves it or denies it. **Once a manager has approved the estimate it says nothing**: the price stays, and a change goes through **✎ Edit estimate** or a change order.
- **It never refuses and never switches.** Save Client, Save Changes, Save Estimate and the manager's PIN all go ahead with it on screen. The switch is a person's, through the service pickers that already exist (Edit Client's *Service Type*, or Build Estimate's service dropdown, which asks first), within the estate family. Measured on Build Estimate, on a 3,200 sq ft house with five rooms ticked at their defaults (an estate opens on a fixed price, so this is the suggested fee): **$24,062.50** as Probate Estate Settlement, **$19,560** once switched to Estate Settlement.
- **The price follows the service, unchanged.** A Probate service on a trust prices exactly what it priced before 2026-10-05, its court step and its 25% fixed-price contingency included (35% on Contested Probate). Nothing reprices a job until somebody switches the service.
- **Contested Probate is never flagged.** A fight over a trust usually reaches a court too, so that work is real and its price stays ($36,990 on the same house). Only its words follow the matter.
- **The documents never claim a probate, on either service.** On such a matter every client document names Probate Estate Settlement **Estate Settlement**, and Contested Probate Estate Settlement **Contested Estate Settlement**: both agreement forms' title and header sentence (§8), the client estimate's *Service* row, its fee-table heading and the Probate narrative under *How We Work* (§7), every invoice's *Service* row (§12), and the estimate, agreement and invoice emails. One namer names the service for all of them, off the catalogue the screens read. **An approved Probate estimate on a trust stops printing "Probate" too**, and its price stays the Probate service's. The screens (the client list, the dashboard, Build Estimate's picker) and the internal emails keep the service's own name, because there it is the price basis.

### The documentation standard — two gates, and they only go one way

Under the estate block, on all three decedent services. Both are **facts somebody knows**, not judgment calls — ask the attorney or the personal representative rather than guessing:

| Question | Answers | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **Federal estate tax return (Form 706) being filed?** | Unknown (the default) · Yes · No | **Unknown counts as YES.** Yes or unknown forces Formal documentation. Only an explicit *No* lifts it. Decedent services only — a downsizing cannot owe estate tax. Since 2026-10-03 (P19) the return's due date, nine months after death, is kept in view on the schedule strip and the Probate or Trust card (§9a-i). |
| **Known dispute, contest, or elective share?** | No / not known · Yes | Forces Formal *and* drops the specialist-appraisal threshold from $3,000 to **$500 per item**. Contested Probate already counts as disputed whatever this says; tick it when an *uncontested* matter turns. |

The live readout under them names the level, why it is that level, and what that level actually gates. On **Formal** (Strict Mode): every flagged item must be appraised or waived before the Court Inventory or the Trust Schedule comes off **DRAFT**, the appraisal guardrail blocks rather than prompts, chain of custody is mandatory, and the client estimate carries the court-grade records list. On **Standard** a flagged item is an amber prompt rather than a block and no schedule is held for one. Both branches close by naming the **specialist threshold** — $3,000 an item, or $500 where a dispute or a contest has lowered it.

> **⚠⚠ CORRECTED 2026-09-22 — it used to name two numbers and the level moved neither.** The readout said *"Standard lists individually above $1,000 and routes to a specialist at $3,000; Formal lists above $100"*. Driven on the real gate chain: the **listing floor** was the only figure that moved with the level, and it was enforced by **nothing** — `invListingThreshold` had exactly one reader in 34,000 lines, this sentence, so the only thing the app ever did with an itemisation floor was tell you it had one. The **specialist threshold** is real and enforced, and is moved by the **dispute** rather than by the level: **$3,000 on a Strict estate and $3,000 on a Standard one**. So the amber box that exists to explain a forced level explained it with one fiction and one number the level is not responsible for. The function and its two constants are retired; see Section 10a. **⚠ The $100 came back the same day as something else entirely** — Treas. Reg. §20.2031-6(a), the federal cap on what may be grouped as one lot on a Form 706 schedule, gated on the **706 answer** and not on this level. It is not this box’s business and this box does not mention it; see *The grouping cap* in Section 10a.

> **Escalate-only, and the dropdown says so rather than pretending.** The gates set a *floor*. The *Documentation level* control may raise the level above that floor and can never lower it — you are always allowed to be stricter and never looser, which is the same logic that makes an unknown 706 count as a yes. When the gates have already forced Formal the dropdown **disables itself and states the reason on screen**. A control that appears to lower it and silently does not would be worse than none.

> **⚠ Strict Mode used to be a one-way door, and it is not any more (2026-09-21).** The readout tells you to *"answer it with the attorney or PR to lift this"* — and until now there was nowhere to answer it: the gates were on the intake form only, so an estate whose 706 question went unanswered on the call stayed in Strict Mode for the life of the job. **Edit Client carries both gates now**, on every estate service, with the same readout. The approval lock does not hold them: it holds only the inputs that price the hours (service type, square footage, years in home, Premium and a Home Transition's new-home square footage), and holding a 706 answer would make a wrong one permanent the moment an estimate was approved.

### What's in the house — two questions on every intake call

New 2026-09-10, replacing the single free-text *Notes* box as the main thing intake asks about the property. Two questions, then a seven-row checklist — tick what is in the house and write down what you are told:

> **Home Prep for Sale asks the safety question and the *Access & security* row** (Anthony, Q17, 2026-09-30; from 2026-09-22 to then it asked the safety question only). Vendors go through the house, so the alarm, the keys and who else comes and goes matter; nothing leaves it, so the must-find question, the other six rows and the *Documentation level* dropdown stay hidden. An answer typed into a hidden row before switching the service to Home Prep is **not saved** onto the job. Home Prep is the first option in the service dropdown.

| Row | What to write in the notes box |
| --- | --- |
| **Cash** — loose cash, envelopes, hidden spots | Approx amount, and where. |
| **Valuables** — jewellery, gold/silver, watches, coins, art, collectibles | What, where, who gets it, appraisal wanted? |
| **Firearms & ammunition** | How many, where, and who is authorized to take possession. |
| **Safes & lockboxes** | Location, do you have the combination or key, are the contents known? |
| **Documents & digital** — will/trust, deeds, insurance, statements, passports, plus crypto wallets, thumb drives, seed phrases, old laptops and phones | What you are looking for, and where it might be. |
| **Sentimental must-finds** — photos, letters, specific heirlooms | Name them. |
| **Access & security** — alarm and codes, cameras, smart locks, who else has keys (caregivers, cleaners, family), gated or HOA | Codes, contact, anything we need to disarm. |

> **The answers do not stay on the intake form — they become the crew's standing job flags.** Everything ticked, plus both free-text answers, renders as a **Standing job flags** panel at the top of the **Job Plan** (§11) and on the client dashboard. On the Job Plan, firearms is the one exception: it is the red banner above the client line rather than a row in the panel (since 2026-09-20 the panel there no longer repeats it); the dashboard has no banner, so its panel keeps the red row. The same brief shows in the room workspace (without the red row, which the workspace pins at its top, where scrolling cannot move it), and its two intake answers alone — the must-find and the safety question — show above the Job Admin & Inv desk. The Job Plan's *Before Day 1* stage carries the checkbox *"Crew confirmed by name and briefed — standing job flags read aloud"* (it was Phase 0's until the stages were named on 2026-09-19); this brief is what it reads from. Asking a widow on the phone where her husband kept the cash and then not telling the two people emptying the house is worse than never asking — the person who asked believes it was passed on.

> **Question 1 is one line per item (new 2026-09-19).** The crew ticks these off in the house, and a paragraph cannot be ticked. Each line of the answer becomes its own row on the brief with a **Found it** tick (§11); the hint on the box says so on intake and on Edit Client. A line typed twice is one row.

> **Firearms prints red and carries the standing rule with it**, above every other flag on the brief: *nothing moves without written authority; photograph it where it lies; tell the concierge the same day; let nobody carry one out, family included.* It is the only red row, and it should stay the only one — red means stop and do not touch, and a second red row costs the first one its meaning. This is the same rule the Job Admin & Inv tab enforces on the item itself (§10a), reached a fortnight earlier. **The row links the full firearms protocol** from 2026-09-13 — the procedure a crew reads standing in the house.

> **A row ticked with no note still reaches the crew, and says so:** *"Ticked at intake, no detail recorded — ask the client before Day 1."* Silence there would be indistinguishable from never having asked. A row left unticked prints nothing at all, and a job where none of this was recorded — anything created before 2026-09-10 — renders no panel rather than an empty one.

> **Notes survives, demoted.** The two questions and seven prompts cover the property; they do not cover family dynamics, urgency, or who actually makes the decisions. That is what the general *Notes* box at the bottom of the card is still for, and it shows on the client's dashboard, along the foot of the client card. (Not on the client list: the detail row meant to carry it there was never on the page, and was deleted on 2026-09-29.) **Edit Client carries all of it** — both questions and the whole checklist, built from the same list, so the two forms cannot end up asking different questions about the same house. Correct it there the moment a client tells you something new.

**Referral source:** Intake records how the job came in. Professional referral types (attorney, realtor, trust officer, etc.) link to a specific **Referral Partner** from that directory, so referrals are attributed on the partner leaderboard. Personal sources (family, friend, existing client) use a free-text "Referred by" name instead. **The form saves what it shows** (2026-09-30): a partner picked and then hidden by switching to a personal source is not saved, and a job records its partner by their directory id rather than their row on the sheet, so sorting the Referral Partners sheet never moves a referral onto somebody else on the leaderboard. (A partner row with no id yet still falls back to its row number: run `backfillIds()` once in the Referral Partners Apps Script project — §14a.) Both are correctable on Edit Client (below).

> **The partner doesn't have to be in the directory yet — *+ New* beside the picker adds them without leaving intake.** Name, firm, phone, email; it writes to the Referral Partners sheet through the same `addPartner` path the directory tab uses, then selects them on the form. A referral arrives from someone new more often than not, and leaving intake to go and add them first is precisely how the attribution gets dropped. The rest of the prospecting detail (partner type, rating, contact history) is desk work on the Referral Partners tab afterwards — §14.

> **A second client at the same street address is named, never refused (Q16, 2026-09-30).** Under the address: *Already on file at this address: …* with the service and status. The same house comes back (a second service, a family member ringing again, a lost lead revived) and each is legitimate; if it is the same client, open them from the dashboard instead. Case, punctuation, the unit and the common suffixes (*Boulevard* / *Blvd*) are ignored, and the zip must agree where both are recorded.

> **Two things the form no longer does to you (2026-09-30).** A start date after the hard target, or before the walkthrough, is **kept** and named in red under the dates; it used to be cleared without a word. And picking an estate service by mistake no longer wipes the phone and email you had typed: they are put aside while the estate is picked and come back when you switch back. The Clear button and a save leave nothing of the last client on the form, the documentation level's borrowed answer and the date pickers' minimums included.

Drive folder structure created automatically per job:

```
Estate Inventory · As-Found Record · Walkthrough Notes · Estimates · Agreement · Change Orders · Invoice
```

Item photos, collection photos/appraisals and the inventory workbook live in **Estate Inventory**; the **as-found** shots have their own **As-Found Record** subfolder since 2026-09-20 (§10a, §15). *Share w/ Counsel* grants read-only access to **both**, so it is still one action and one package. Everything else — Walkthrough Notes, Estimates, Agreement, Change Orders, Invoice — stays private. A folder made by the older fallback route (used only when the app's normal request to the script fails outright) names its estimate folder *Estimate* and adds an empty *Job Log*; the app files into either name.

### Correcting a client later — Edit Client (brought up to intake's rules 2026-09-30)

**✎ Edit Client** on the client's dashboard is where everything intake asked is corrected. Since 2026-09-30 (workflow audit pack P9) it follows the same rules as the form that created the client:

- **It never erases what it cannot show.** The concierge list is the active roster (a concierge added on the Contractors tab is on it), plus whoever the job already names, marked *not on the active roster* if they have left it. The representative's role list is intake's own list, the three court-appointed roles included and, since 2026-10-01, no *Power of Attorney*. A role, referral source or partner the lists no longer carry is kept and marked *as recorded*. Before this, the concierge list was three typed-in names (one of them a specialist) and the role list lacked *Administrator ad litem*, *Curator* and *Guardian ad litem*, so saving the modal without touching anything **erased** a directory concierge or an ad litem role.
- **The same required fields as intake.** A save that would clear a required field is refused and names it. A field that was already blank on an older client does not stop the save: it is named afterwards (*Saved. Still missing on this client: …*), so a phone number can still be corrected on a job nobody has finished.
- **The service stays in its family.** A living client is offered only the living services and an estate only the estate services; the save refuses anything else with *Create a new client for this*. Re-typing a living client as an estate kept their own phone and email where the representative's belong (and the reverse printed *Estate of …* on a living client's estimate).
- **An estate asks nothing of the deceased.** No phone or email field is shown for them; the representative's are below. The one ladder that addresses every email and every DocuSign envelope (the client, then the representative, then counsel) now **skips the client on an estate**, so a signature request can never go out in the deceased's name, even if an old save left an email on their record. Where only counsel has an email recorded, the estate attorney is the envelope's Client signer, and **since 2026-10-06 (P22) the DocuSign send's notice says so, in amber**: *"Ann Lowe, the estate attorney, was sent the Client’s signature line: no email is recorded for Rex Hale. If the representative should sign, void the envelope in DocuSign, add their email on Edit Client and send again."* Who signs there is unchanged, pending Anthony.
- **Dates out of order are kept and named in red.** A start after the hard target, or before the walkthrough, stays as typed; the line under the dates says which is out of order. Both forms used to **clear** the hard target or the start silently, and the save wrote the blank. Weekends are still refused as they are picked.
- **Fields only intake could set:** years in home (it prices the job, so it is held once the estimate is approved, like the square footage), bedrooms, full and half baths (the walkthrough's coverage badge reads them), and the referral source and partner (Q15). The Letters date has been here since 2026-09-29.
- **A held field names the route (2026-09-30).** Once the estimate is approved, the inputs that price the hours are held and the save says so — and since 2026-10-06 (P22) while it is out for manager approval too, when the alert says the manager is deciding on that price; the alert now names the way through in the state the estimate is in — **✎ Edit estimate** (it un-approves the estimate, after which the field can be changed here) until the signing packet goes out, a change order after it, the manager's decision while it is out for approval. It used to say *raise a change order* in every state. Build Estimate's refusal on an approved estimate names the same route.
- **The documentation tier moves a draft estimate, never an approved one (2026-09-30).** See §4: the estimate open on Build Estimate follows the new tier at once, a saved one when it is next opened, and an approved one is flagged with the route instead.
- **Notes keep their lines (2026-09-30).** The notes box is a multi-line box, as at intake, and the dashboard shows the lines as typed. It was a one-line box, which dropped a note's line breaks the first time anybody saved a correction here.
- **Home Prep** shows the access & security row and the safety question, as intake does (below); an answer on a hidden row stays on the record rather than being read off a control nobody could see.
- **The trust, its paper and its co-representatives (2026-10-03, P19).** The authority control is named for the matter's paper (the Letters, or the Certification of Trust) and is hidden where the matter has none, switching as you change *How is this estate being administered?*; *Trust Details* and *Property Sale Required* follow the same answer, and the save writes them only where they are shown. *Co-representatives* lists everyone recorded with the representative: correct a row, **+ Add a co-representative**, or **✕ Remove** one, which asks first and takes effect when you press Save Changes (Cancel keeps them). A row you did not touch is not written again, and a row with no name is refused.
- **A Probate service on a matter with no court is flagged (2026-10-05, P20).** Under *How is this estate being administered?*, on open and as you change the service or the matter, and again in the notice after Save Changes, until the estimate is approved (*A Probate service on a matter with no court*, above). It never refuses the save, and the service changes only if you change it.

## 5. Estimate (Labor-Based Services)

**Screen: Build Estimate** → opened from the client's own timeline with the brown **Build estimate** button (or **✎ Edit estimate** beside the estimate once one exists, until the signing packet goes out — and not while a manager has it for approval). It is a full screen off the Client Dashboard as of 2026-09-23, not a tab, and it is always for that one client — named in the bar across the top, whose **←** button returns you to them (§1). This section covers the labor-based services (Home Editing, Home Transition, Home Cleanout, Estate Settlement, Probate and Contested Probate). Home Prep for Sale uses the fee-only flow in Section 6.

> **⚠ There is no job dropdown on Build Estimate any more (2026-09-23).** The tab had a job picker (from 2026-09-19 a Won job with a saved estimate came off it, because picking an approved estimate out of it only ever landed on a locked form). With the screen opened *from* the client there is nothing to pick, and the way into a saved estimate is **✎ Edit estimate** beside it on the client's dashboard, which un-approves it first; it stays offered until the signing packet goes out (above), because the rule is the client's acceptance, not the manager's approval. **Clear** became **Start over**, on the same client.

**How the screen is laid out** (rebuilt 2026-08-02, so a screenshot older than that will not match):

| Band | Holds |
| --- | --- |
| **Top card, two columns** | *Job* on the left — the client's name with **Start over**, **service type (a dropdown since 2026-09-10, not a label — §4)**, target start date (read-only; set it with Edit Client), estimate prepared by, and on a Home Transition the Move Management card (§5c). *Schedule & Crew* on the right — the *Concierges* and *Specialists* counts, the concierge hands-on share, the documentation scope (estates), the crew and timeline badges, the *client needs it sooner* planner, and **Rush order**. |
| **Adjustments** | Its own full-width card: difficult access, multiple heirs, premium estate. |
| **Build column** | The Room-by-Room Assessment (sections run **two across** the full page width), then Notable Collections beside Vehicles & Watercraft, then **Third-Party Vendors**, then Moving Materials. *Changed 2026-09-10:* the vendor card was at the *top* of this column and is now below the collections, because that is the order the questions can actually be answered in — it is the collections and the vehicles that tell you whether this job needs an auction house, a gemologist or a boat appraiser at all. *Changed 2026-08-03:* the separate Home Prep card that used to sit beside Moving Materials is gone — prep vendors are entered in the *Property Preparation* card of Third-Party Vendors with every other vendor (§5e). |
| **Bottom** | Estimate Summary, then Pricing Reference Check beside the discount and fixed-price levers (with a one-line reminder of any rush premium), then Reset / Save. |

> **The discount and the fixed price sit under the totals they move, and that is deliberate.** They are read against the number, not against the scope — they spent a spell up beside Adjustments and were three scrolls away from anything they changed. Scope decisions live at the top; price decisions live at the bottom next to the price. **Rush lives in Schedule & Crew** (since 2026-08-03), because it is a timing decision: its premium lands on the summary, and a reminder under the discount points back up to it.

> **The Estimate Summary on this screen is condensed.** The hours table (§5i), the blended-rate bar and the internal margin panel are hidden here and shown nowhere else; the app still calculates them on every recalculation, so the client estimate and the invoices are unchanged. On an hourly quote the **Transition Concierge** and **Property Specialist** fee rows stay, each with a grey line saying what its hours are made of (§5b); on a fixed price the fee replaces them (below). The client estimate (§7) prints the concierge and specialist hours × rate, never the breakdown. *Corrected 2026-09-30:* this note used to say all of it reappeared on the client estimate. See §16 for what this means for the margin panel specifically.

> **The Estimate Summary shows what the client is charged, on the basis you chose (2026-09-30, P12).** On a fixed price its first row is the **Fixed project fee** and the hourly fee rows under it are hidden; the first total is the **Services subtotal** (the fee or the hourly services, plus the 30% prep fee), then **Expedited delivery**, then the **Preferred Client Discount**, in the order every document prints them, and **Total project estimate** is the figure the client is quoted. Until today it showed the hourly figures under a fixed quote: a $21,840 fixed price read *Total project estimate $18,200*.

### 5a. Walkthrough Notes & Voice

During the site visit, use the **📝** (notes) and **🎙** (voice-to-text) buttons in each room row. Type with **📝** and press **Save Notes**: the note goes on the room and a `.txt` copy is filed to the job's **Walkthrough Notes** Drive folder at once. **🎙** transcribes in real time straight into the same note, with no Save Notes step. Either way the note is saved with the estimate when you press **Save Estimate**, which also files every room and collection note to Walkthrough Notes (with a `.json` sidecar of the walkthrough) and the internal Estimate Worksheet to the job's Estimates folder.

**Private Walkthrough Notes** is a separate box, sitting as the last cell of the room grid with its own 🎙 button. It is *internal only* — never shown to the client, never on the estimate document, never uploaded to a shared folder. It is for what you would not say in front of the family: access problems, hoarding, who is actually making the decision, anything that changes how the job runs. It saves with the estimate (`est.privateNote`) and reloads with it. The intended use is dictating in the car on the way out.

> **⚠⚠ IT NOW HAS A READER, AND UNTIL 2026-09-19 IT HAD NONE.** This box had exactly two readers in the whole app — itself, and the line that writes it back into itself when you reopen the estimate. So the one thing most worth re-reading before Day 1 was reachable only by opening a form that is *locked* by then. It is now on the client dashboard under **🔍 Walkthrough** (§9c), at the top of the page, above the rooms. **Nothing about where it goes has changed**: still internal, still never on the client estimate, still never in a shared Drive folder, and the page it renders on cannot be sent, filed or printed.

> 📷 Photos are NOT captured during the estimate stage. Photo capture opens once the job is **Won** (§10): a shot taken before that is not saved, and the camera says why. *Corrected 2026-09-30:* this note said *only after job activation*, which has not been the rule since 2026-08-03.

### 5b. Room Scoring

Check each room to include it. Set Volume and Complexity on a 1–5 scale:

| Score | Volume | Complexity |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Nearly empty | Simple / standard items |
| 3 | Moderately filled | Mix of standard and care items |
| 5 | Completely full | High-value / fragile / specialty handling |

Check **Special Items** if the room contains artwork, antiques, or items requiring specialty care: it adds 1 to that room's complexity (to 5 at most). **Typing a volume of 5 leaves the room's complexity as it is** (since 2026-10-01, P17; until then a hand-typed 5 set the complexity to 5 too, and a room that was full but ordinary had to be lowered again by hand). Hours and a working-day timeline generate automatically as you score. **A room row shows that room's share of the hands-on work pool** — sorting, documentation, packing and haul-out together — split between the concierge's hands-on share (*TC*) and the crew's (*PS*). Once the walkthrough is complete the PS column adds up to the Property Specialist fee line, give or take the rounding up to the whole hour (the difficult-access uplift and any collections handling sit outside the rooms). The TC column does not add up to the concierge line, and is not meant to: that line also carries the concierge's on-site supervision and off-site coordination, which belong to no room (§5i; the Drive worksheet shows them as *Job-level work*). Measured on a 3,500 sqft Estate Settlement with seventeen rooms scored at their defaults: 96.4 PS in the rows against 97 billed, 24.4 TC against 65 (re-measured 2026-10-02, P18, with billed hours rounded up to the whole hour again; 96.5 and 63.5 for P17's one day of nearest quarter hours). *Corrected 2026-09-30:* this said the rows reconcile to both fee lines. How the totals become billed concierge and specialist hours is Section 5i.

> **Corrected 2026-08-03 — this section used to say a room row was "the packing step only", and it was.** Packing is 27% of the hands-on pool on an estate job, so every row understated its own room by **3.7×**: a 2-car garage displayed *2.4 PS* and booked **9.1 person-hours**. Nothing on the screen let you feel a quote building as you ticked rooms, and a 3,500 sqft house priced at $27,000 with nobody able to see where it came from. The rows carry the full pool now, so **the old advice to mentally multiply a room row is obsolete.** The one residual left is the concierge's supervision and off-site time, which belong to no room and are itemized under the fee line (below).

> **Rooms are still not comparable between service types.** The same closet costs different hours on a home editing job than on a probate one, because the pool coefficients differ per service — Estate Settlement runs **2.2×** the hands-on hours of a Home Editing job on the same house, and Contested Probate **1.5×** an Estate Settlement. That is a deliberate policy ratio, not a scoring difference. Compare a room to other rooms on the same job, never across jobs of different types.

> **"How full is this house?" — one press instead of nineteen (NEW 2026-08-03).** A chip row above the room grid: **Seasonal · Light · Normal · Full · Packed**. It sets the starting volume for every room *in scope* in one go. *Normal* is the behaviour that was there before, so ignoring the row changes nothing.
>
> Each preset is a **shift on each room's own default, never a blanket value** — that part is load-bearing. A foyer and a powder room open at 1 because they are inherently lighter whatever the house is like, so setting everything to 5 would claim a powder room is as full as a garage. On *Packed* the foyer is still below the living room. Pressing the same chip twice lands on the same numbers, and pressing a different one re-bases from the defaults rather than compounding. It exists because telling the app a house was packed used to mean moving nineteen sliders by hand, so nobody did it and every house priced as average.
>
> **⚠ It is a STANDING setting, and it was not until 2026-09-10.** The chip used to apply *once*, to whatever rooms were ticked at the moment it was pressed — so on the natural walkthrough order (stand in the first room, say the place is packed, then work through the house) **every room ticked after the press reverted to the bare defaults**. A room sitting at 3 because the preset never reached it looks exactly like a room somebody meant to leave at 3, and because volume is averaged over the rooms scored and applied to the whole square footage, the under-scoring moves the price of the *entire job* rather than of those rooms. Nothing on the screen could ever have shown it. Now the active chip seeds every room you tick from then on, so you can press it before you have ticked anything — which is the sensible opening move.
>
> **⚠ And a room you score by HAND is never overwritten.** The moment you type a volume into a room, that room is yours: a later chip re-bases every other room around it and leaves yours alone, and the feedback line says how many it held back. That is the right way round — your number is a direct observation, somebody standing in the room looking at it, where the chip is a guess about the property as a whole. **To hand a room back to the preset, untick and re-tick it** (leaving scope clears the score, so the two can never disagree). The flag rides the saved estimate, so reopening a quote remembers which rooms were observed; *an estimate saved before 2026-09-10 carries none, so a chip re-bases the lot — which is exactly how it behaved on the day it was priced.*
>
> **It does not touch complexity**, and wiring "premium estate" to complexity was considered and rejected: complexity moves a job about **2.5%** while the premium toggle moves it **24%** (re-measured 2026-10-02, P18, on a 3,500 sqft Estate Settlement, seven rooms: $18,000 at their defaults and $18,450 at complexity 5, then $22,905 with Premium on; 3% and 24% on P17's quarter hours, the toggle moved it 48% until P17 took Premium's 25 hours out, and these read 3.9% and 49.6% on the engine of 2026-09-30), so you would never notice it fire — and it would collapse two separate questions, *how careful* and *how expensive*, into one.

**Where the rest of the hours are stated (changed 2026-08-03).** There used to be a *Project coordination & logistics* strip under the room grid carrying the residual. It is gone: it named the largest block of work on an estate job — mostly documentation and triage — as though it were administrative overhead, and it gave a number without ever saying what was in it. The same hours are now itemized by work step directly under the **Transition Concierge fee** and **Property Specialist fee** rows in the Estimate Summary, beside the figures they explain (on an hourly quote; on a fixed price the two rows, and the breakdown with them, give way to the *Fixed project fee*). The concierge line splits first into *on site with the crew* and *off site*, then names the off-site drivers. Both lines are scaled so the parts add up to the hours printed on the same row.

The two label sets are deliberately different. A work step carries two coefficients — off-site coordination and the hands-on pool — and they are not the same work done by two people. So the specialist line says *Packing & handling* while the concierge line says *Packing logistics & materials*: the concierge is ordering crates and scheduling the delivery, not packing. Don't collapse the two lists.

**A ticked room does not start at 3/3 everywhere.** Most rooms open at the neutral 3 — a normally furnished Palm Beach home. Inherently light rooms open at the 1 floor: entryway/foyer, half bath, bathrooms 2–4, additional bathrooms, laundry, mudroom, mechanical/utility. Primary baths open at 2. The starting score is a starting point in every case — **score what you are looking at**.

Room sections launch **collapsed** so the estimate opens compact, and run **two across** the page — open each section as you walk that part of the house. Use *Expand all / Collapse all / Hide empty* at the top of the assessment; each section header shows a live in-scope count. Reopening a scored estimate auto-reveals the sections that have rooms in scope.

> **Volume and complexity are property-wide, not per-room — which is why a half-finished walkthrough misprices rather than under-counts.** Each room's scores are read against that room's own default and averaged across the rooms you scored, and that average is then applied to the load derived from the *whole* square footage. A room left at its default is neutral, so a few rooms scored at their defaults price the house at Normal. But score one living room at 1/1 on a 10,000 sqft Estate Settlement and nothing else, and the app prices all 10,000 sqft as though the entire house were that empty: **$25,600 against $43,850** (measured 2026-09-30). Until 2026-09-30 a foyer at its own 1/1 did the same — on a real job that took the quote from about $44,900 to $24,100; a foyer at 1/1 is neutral now (below). Half a walkthrough is not half a price; it is a wrong price.

**Room coverage flag.** Under the room grid the app compares the bedrooms and baths *recorded at intake* against the bedrooms and baths you have **accounted for** — scored *or* deliberately marked out of scope — and says so:

| Badge | Means |
| --- | --- |
| Green — *Room coverage matches intake* | Every bedroom and bath on record is accounted for. Where some are out of scope it says which, e.g. "**2 scored, 5 out of scope**" — it never claims a room you excluded was walked. |
| Red — *Walkthrough looks incomplete* | Names the shortfall, e.g. "accounted for **2 of 5** bedrooms, **1 of 4** full baths". Finish the walkthrough, mark the rooms Havellin is not touching as out of scope, or knowingly accept that the unscored rooms are being priced off the ones you did score. |

> **The Scope column has THREE states, and an excluded room is accounted for — not missing (fixed 2026-09-10).** Tapping a room cycles it **blank → in scope (✓) → homeowner-handled / out of scope (✕) → blank**. Until this fix the badge was only shown the ✓ rooms, so a room you had deliberately marked ✕ counted exactly the same as one nobody had opened — and a job that is mostly Home Prep, where the guest wing, the kitchen and the laundry are legitimately the client's own, read *"Walkthrough looks incomplete"* at a walkthrough that was finished. The only way to clear it was to price rooms Havellin is not touching. **The badge is red rather than amber now**, because a half-scored house mis*prices* rather than under-counts, and a warning that fires on a normal job teaches people to ignore the one check that catches the expensive case.

> **An out-of-scope room no longer blocks Save or Submit (fixed 2026-09-10, same change).** That test existed in *three* places and they disagreed: the manager-PIN approval skipped excluded rooms, while *Save* and *Submit for Approval* did not — so a walkthrough with any ✕ room could be approved but never saved. It was a **deadlock, not a nag**: an excluded row has its volume and complexity inputs *disabled and cleared*, so the app was demanding a 1–5 score in a field it had greyed out, naming rooms the estimator had put out of scope on purpose. All three now read one shared test (`unscoredRoomNames`). Excluded rooms still ride the estimate at **$0** so they print on the client estimate and the job plan as explicitly not ours — that zero is the record of a decision, not a gap.

It is a *flag, not a gate* — a concierge mid-walkthrough knows the estimate is incomplete and does not need to be stopped. Saving with *zero* rooms is already refused, which is the case worth blocking. The check goes quiet if intake never captured bed/bath counts, so fill those in at intake (§4) or this check has nothing to compare against.

> **The badge counts grid rows, so intake can only ask for what the grid can reach.** Each bedroom and bath is counted by matching the room's name against the rows you scored, which means the highest number reachable for a given kind is the number of *rows carrying that name* — not the number of rooms the house has. Reachable today: **8 bedrooms and 14 full baths**, all *main-house* rows across the First Floor and Second Floor sections, and **5 half baths**: the four *Half Bath* rows and the *Pool / Cabana Half Bath* (below). Until 2026-08-03 *Half Bath* existed in exactly one section, so the half-bath count was capped at **1** and any property with two powder rooms stayed flagged no matter how completely it was walked. If a count is ever raised at intake beyond what the grid can reach, the badge becomes unclearable and stops meaning anything — add the rows in the same change.

**Where the half baths are.** *Half Bath* now appears in **Entry & Living** (the powder room off the foyer), **Kitchen & Utility**, and both **First Floor** and **Second Floor Bedrooms & Bathrooms**. A fifth, **Pool / Cabana Half Bath**, sits in **Exterior & Auxiliary** — it is an exterior room, so it adds load on top of the under-air baseline rather than modulating it (§5i), which is right for a cabana bath that is not inside the square footage. All five open at 1/1 and count toward the intake half-bath tally.

*Additional Bathroom(s)* is still classified as a **full** bath, and it is a plural row counted once — so a house with several surplus baths can still read short. Use the dedicated half-bath rows for powder rooms rather than pressing *Additional Bathroom(s)* into service, which moves the full-bath count up while leaving the half-bath count where it was.

> **Intake's bed and bath counts are the MAIN HOUSE, and outbuildings no longer count toward them (changed 2026-08-03).** A guest house bedroom used to satisfy the intake figure, so scoring four main bedrooms plus a guest house and a pool house against an intake of six turned the badge *green* with two main bedrooms never walked — a false clear on the one check that catches a half-scored house. Outbuilding rows are off both coverage lists. Enter main-house counts at intake; capture the guest quarters on the walkthrough (§5b, Outbuildings & Guest Quarters). Putting a casita's bedroom into the intake figure now makes the badge unclearable, because the grid has no outbuilding bedroom row to score against it.

#### Outbuildings & guest quarters — one row per building

Detached buildings are scored as **whole buildings sized by bedroom count**, not room by room. Until 2026-08-03 this section carried eleven rows (each building split into bedroom / living / kitchen / bath), which is a level of detail nobody uses on a detached structure — you score a guest house 3/3 as a whole and move on. The rows now are:

| Row | Weight | Replaces |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Guest House — 1 / 2 / 3 bedroom | 7.0 / 9.0 / 11.0 | Guest House bedroom + living + kitchen/bar + bath, then +2.0 a bedroom |
| Caretaker's Cottage — 1 / 2 / 3 bedroom | 5.5 / 7.5 / 9.5 | Cottage bedroom + living/kitchen + bath, then +2.0 a bedroom |
| Additional Outbuilding Room | 2.0 | unchanged — still a free-text row |

Each weight is the **sum of the sub-rooms it replaces**, so a fully-ticked building prices exactly as it did before; only the number of clicks changed. Every additional bedroom adds 2.0, the old bedroom weight — keep that step if a size is ever added.

> **ONE pool house, one row, and it assumes living quarters (changed 2026-09-18). It is not in this section — it is in Exterior & Auxiliary.** There used to be two rows for one building: a bare *Pool House* in Exterior & Auxiliary (2.7) against three *Pool House — …* sub-rows here (4.5 together). Ticking both double-charged it by about 7 specialist hours nobody would have questioned. They were renamed apart on 2026-08-03 — *Pool House / Cabana — no living quarters* against *Pool House — with living quarters* — which made the pair readable without making it necessary, so on 2026-09-18 it became one row. **The survivor is *Pool House — with living quarters* in Exterior & Auxiliary, and it carries 4.5 rather than the 2.7 that row used to have** — the label promises a bedroom, a kitchen and a bath, so it has to price like one. Measured on a 3,500 sqft Estate Settlement: **12 specialist hours, against 7 at the old 2.7.** A pool house with nothing in it is a changing room — score the *Pool / Cabana Half Bath* row and leave it at that.

> **The casita row is gone (2026-09-18), and what to tick instead depends on what the building actually is.** It moved out of Exterior & Auxiliary into this section on 2026-08-03 and then came out of the grid altogether: at 2.5 it said nothing the pool house and the 1-bedroom guest house either side of it did not, and most people describing a property use *casita* and *guest house* for the same structure. A casita that is a bedroom and a bath is the free-text **Additional Outbuilding Room** row — rename it *Casita* and it prices at **2.0**, half a load unit under the old row, so on a genuinely small one expect roughly one specialist hour less than before. A casita with its own living space or kitchenette is a **Guest House — 1 bedroom** at 7.0. Pick on what is inside it, not on what the owner calls it.

> **⚠ A room is scored against its own default (2026-09-30, P12; Anthony on Q6).** A foyer, a powder room, a laundry and the secondary baths open at 1 because 1 is normal for those rooms, and a primary bath at 2. The price now reads each room against its own starting score, so **a room left at its default never moves the quote**: tick every room the coverage badge asks for and the price stays where it was. Score a room above its default and the house prices fuller; below it, lighter. Until today those light rooms counted as nearly empty against an absolute 3, so finishing the walkthrough *lowered* the quote, by up to 8% on an Estate Settlement. Outbuildings and garages still add their own load on top, as before.
>
> **A score is a whole number from 1 to 5.** Type 9 and the box reads 5; type 2.5 and it reads 3. A box left **blank** (or 0) is **unscored**: it prices at the room's own default meanwhile, and **Save Estimate refuses, naming the room**. Until today a blank or a 0 was quietly priced and saved as a 3, so that refusal could never happen.

### 5c. Job Settings

- **Premium Estate** — TC $185/hr, PS $125/hr (vs. standard $150/$100), **and nothing else since 2026-10-01** (P17, Anthony: *"we should not assume all of these categories for every premium job. just go off the estimate."*). Until then it also added a flat 25 concierge hours of specialty coordination (auction house, appraisers, wine/art logistics) to every Premium estimate, whether or not the job used any of those channels. Each of them is priced by its own vendor line instead (§5d-i), as on every job, so a Premium estimate bills the hours a standard one does, at the higher rates. The toggle's line reads *$185 TC / $125 PS rates only. Coordination comes from the vendor lines you add, as on every job.* (it read *… rates, plus 25 concierge hours of specialty coordination* from 2026-09-30, and *rate only, hours unchanged* before that, which was not true of the engine until today). **P16's rule that an appraiser line on a Premium estate books no hours went with the 25 hours**: the line books its 2.0 hours on every estate, and the vendor card no longer says it is covered by Premium Estate. Measured on a 3,500 sqft Estate Settlement, seven rooms at their defaults: $26,975 (85 TC / 90 PS hours) → $22,350 (60 / 90, the standard estimate's own hours at the Premium rates; re-measured 2026-10-02, P18, on whole hours rounded up, §5i; $22,071.25 at 59 / 89.25 on P17's quarter hours).
- **Crew** — two dropdowns side by side in the **Schedule & Crew** card: *Concierges* (1–2) and *Specialists* (2–6). Both track the engine's recommendation up and down until you set one by hand; from that moment the app leaves it alone and the badge underneath reports what your choice costs.
  > **A crew below the recommendation is now honoured, and priced.** It used to be an undisclosed floor — picking 4 against a recommended 6 changed nothing at all, and the badge then reported the 6 it had actually used against a dropdown still reading 4. Two numbers for one crew, on screen together. A smaller crew is a legitimate answer; it just takes longer, and because the concierge is on site every one of those days the fee goes *up*. The badge names the trade in days: *"2 PS assigned, 6 recommended — the job runs 23 working days instead of 9."*
  > **The estimate says how many specialists, never which.** This was six tick boxes, and before that six dropdowns naming a person or a rate tier per slot — a question nobody can answer three weeks before the job, and the wrong question to ask at estimate time. Naming people happens on the **Job Plan** once the job is Won (§11), where the roster seeds from this count and every row asks for a name. Costing at estimate stage assumes the standard contractor rate for every specialist; the margin firms up as the real people are named.
  > **Crew size moves the price.** Under the working-supervisor model a bigger crew finishes sooner, so the concierge is on site for fewer hours — which makes the estimate *lower*, not higher. That is correct, but it means a crew left over from a larger job will quietly discount this one, so the app flags any crew above the recommendation. Do not carry a crew size over between estimates. See Section 5i. **On a fixed price it does not (2026-09-30, Q5):** the suggested fee is priced as the job would run with two specialists, so a bigger crew (the app's own recommendation on a larger house, or yours) never lowers the fee, and the fixed-price panel says so. The hours, the calendar and the Job Plan still follow the crew you chose. On an hourly job a bigger crew still bills fewer concierge hours, because those are the hours actually worked, and Anthony has confirmed that stays (2026-09-30; §5i). **The badge under the crew says which, since 2026-09-30 (P16).** It told you on every basis that the extra specialist made *"the estimate … lower than it would be at 3 PS"*; on a fixed price it now reads *"… but the suggested fixed fee stays on the two-specialist plan whatever crew runs it, so it shortens the calendar without lowering the fee"*, and below the recommendation *"the fee does not move; only the calendar is longer"* where it said the fee was higher. An hourly quote's badge is unchanged.
- **Client needs it sooner?** — pick the **date** the client asked for. The **timeline planner** underneath answers it directly: what this job delivers as staffed, and what crew reaches that date. Full detail in §5i.
- **Rush order** now lives in this card too, directly under the planner — it is a timing decision, and it was being made three scrolls below the crew size and completion date it depends on. Its effect on the price still appears on the Estimate Summary at the bottom, where a one-line reminder points back up here.
- **Concierges** is a count, not a name. The app sets it to 2 when the daily load passes the cap, and back to 1 when it no longer does, until you set it yourself. Unlike specialists it changes *neither the fee nor the duration* — see the note at the end of §5i — so setting it to 1 against a recommendation of 2 changes no number at all and the hint says so: somebody works a 12-hour day.
- **Concierge hands-on share** (the production rate α; per estimate since 2026-09-02, when it came out of Settings) — the share of the concierge's time on site spent handling contents alongside the crew rather than directing it (§5i). It opens at the firm default set in ⚙ Settings (50%), and **Default** puts it back. Lower it on a trophy estate where the concierge is mostly directing; raise it on a straightforward home editing job where they work the whole day. Higher means fewer specialist hours and a shorter job. It is pinned on the saved estimate, so a Settings change never reprices a quote, and the crew badge says when an estimate is held at a rate other than the current Settings figure.
- **Documentation scope** (estate services only, under the concierge hands-on share; new 2026-09-04) — *Full*, *Capture only*, or *None*. Whether the attorney's office keeps the inventory in-house is decided job by job, so this is a per-estimate control pinned on the saved estimate, like the production rate — never a Settings value and never a service type. **Since 2026-09-21 it is seeded from the engagement tier** recorded at intake (§4) — that is what the agreement promises, and this is the projection of it that prices the job. The walkthrough can still override it here. **Since 2026-09-30 an estimate no manager has approved follows a tier changed later on Edit Client** (§4): at once if it is open here, otherwise when it is next opened, and the hint under the dropdown then says why the price moved — *…changed to Inventory with values after this estimate was saved, so it follows it here: priced at Full, where the saved copy is at Capture only. Save Estimate keeps it.*
  > **The paperwork is roughly a third of an estate ticket, and more on probate.** On a 3,500 sqft home at Normal, priced at two specialists, the `document` step is 32% of an Estate Settlement and 38% of Probate and of Contested Probate (re-measured 2026-09-30, after its appraiser scheduling came out, below; it read 34%, 39% and 41%). Until this control existed it was hard-wired to the service type. Stripping it prices an Estate Settlement within a few hundred dollars of a Home Cleanout on the same house — which is the honest floor if counsel keeps the inventory — while the estate voice, the written-authority gate and the heavier disposition rules all stay, because those come from the service type, not from the scope.
  > **What each setting prices.** *Full* is the original: Havellin catalogues every item of consequence with an estimated value — all of the documentation work, and half of the step's scheduling column, *Inventory scheduling* (below). Both valued tiers price here; which one decides who engages the appraisers (§4, and below). *Capture only* is Havellin photographing, describing and locating each item and handing the *list* to counsel, whose people value it and arrange any appraisal — half the documentation work and none of its scheduling (the 50% is a starting coefficient, to be tuned on the first real job). *None* removes the step entirely. On a service with no documentation step (home editing, home transition, home cleanout, prep) the control is hidden and the field is ignored. On an hourly quote the breakdown under the Property Specialist fee relabels the halved step *Photograph & list (counsel values)*.
  > **⚠ An appraiser is priced only by its own vendor line (2026-09-30, Anthony on Q20: *"If we need an appraiser, don't we just add one? It's not mandatory and shouldn't be blocked either."*).** The step's scheduling column was *Appraiser & inventory scheduling*, and *Full* priced all of it — so the *Inventory with values* tier charged for appraiser scheduling that counsel does on that tier, and an estimate that also carried an appraiser line paid for the appraiser twice. It is *Inventory scheduling* now and *Full* prices half of it (a starting figure, like the capture share); *Capture only* still prices none. An appraiser is priced when you add one under Vendors (Asset Liquidation & Valuation: 4 touches, 2 concierge hours, §5d-i; the appraiser's own fee is pass-through like any vendor's), on any tier, and no tier requires or refuses one. The hints under the control say so. Measured on a 3,500 sqft house (eight rooms at their defaults): Estate Settlement $19,900 → $19,450, Probate $23,050 → $22,750, Contested Probate $31,200 → $30,150; the living-client services are unchanged. An estimate saved before today prints what it was saved at, and prices under this rule when it is next opened on Build Estimate. **On the *Inventory + appraisals* tier with no appraiser on the estimate**, the hint adds an amber line — *"This tier promises the specialist appraisals: add each appraiser under Vendors, so its scheduling is priced on its own line."* — a flag, never a refusal. **On a Premium Estate it books them too, since 2026-10-01 (P17).** For one day (P16, 2026-09-30) it booked none, on the theory that Premium's 25 hours covered appraiser coordination; the 25 hours are gone (the Premium bullet above), so the line's own 2.0 hours are its only booking, at the Premium rate. Measured on a 3,500 sqft house, seven rooms at their defaults, a $1,500 Art Appraiser line, Premium: Estate Settlement hourly $22,350 → $22,720, its suggested fixed fee $26,820 → $27,264, Probate hourly $26,135 → $26,505; without Premium, $18,000 → $18,300 on the same Estate Settlement (re-measured 2026-10-02, P18, on whole hours; on P17's quarter hours $22,071.25 → $22,441.25, $26,485.50 → $26,929.50, $25,825 → $26,195 and $17,775 → $18,075).
  > **The client estimate and the estate agreement both read the pin, not the catalogue.** Stage 2 becomes *Sorting, Photography & Listing* or *Sorting & Set-Aside*, the records list drops the valued inventory (and the appraisals, unless the estimate lists an appraiser), and the agreement's scope paragraph, §5.2 compliance list, midpoint-payment trigger and appraisal row all follow (§7, §8) — except who arranges the appraisals. That follows the **tier** at *Full* (§4, 2026-09-24) and, since 2026-09-30, the estimate's own appraiser lines: on the *Inventory + appraisals* tier Havellin arranges every appraisal; on any other tier an appraiser line means Havellin coordinates the appraisals Exhibit A lists, and counsel (or the trustee, or the client) arranges any other; with neither, counsel arranges them all. An estimate saved before 2026-09-04 carries no scope and is read as *Full* — that is what it was priced at.
- **Difficult access** (stairs, no elevator) — **+15% to the hands-on work pool only**. It makes the physical work take longer, which lengthens the job, which lengthens concierge presence automatically. Lifting the concierge line as well would double-count it.
- **Multiple heirs / decision-makers** — **+20% to off-site coordination only**. More calls and sign-offs, not more boxes. Baked into contested-probate rates already, so the toggle greys out on those jobs.
  > **It is a full 20%, but of off-site coordination — not of total concierge hours.** Total TC is on-site presence *plus* off-site coordination, and the uplift touches only the second, so on the total concierge line it lands as roughly **5–6% on an estate or a Home Cleanout and 9–11% on Home Editing or Home Transition**, because off-site coordination is about a quarter of the concierge's hours on the first and half or more on the second (measured on a 3,500 sqft house, 2026-09-30; this note used to say 10% for every job). This gets reported as a bug about once a rebuild; the arithmetic has always been right and the toggle's own label was the thing that was wrong. It now names the pool it lifts, and since 2026-09-30 how big that pool is on the service in hand: *about half of total concierge hours* on Home Editing and Home Transition, *about a quarter* on Home Cleanout and the estates (it said *about half* on every service).
- **Move Management** (Home Transition only, in the Job column) — reads the new home's square footage from the client's record (set it with Edit Client; Save refuses without it, §5h) and prices move day off it: the concierge 8 hours plus 0.002 of an hour per new-home sqft, the specialists 0.009 of an hour per sqft for unpacking. **Art hanging / window treatments / styling?** adds 8 specialist hours. The unpacking adds working days after the crew days (§5i), and the client estimate prints it under *Move Management — Destination Setup*.
- **Rush order** — adds a flat **20% expedited-delivery premium** to the Havellin services — never to the 30% home prep fee (Q9, below) — shown to the client as its own line. *Corrected 2026-09-30:* this said *to the Havellin services total*, and so did the toggle's subtitle, which now reads *Flat 20% premium on the Havellin services, never on the home prep fee, shown as its own line*. It is a premium on the price, deliberately *not* a markup on the TC/PS hourly rates: rates outside NASMM norms read badly to a client comparing quotes, and a named line is clearer and easier to defend. It is also exactly what they're buying: **priority scheduling** to meet the timeline they asked for. The line under it on the estimate and the invoices says so, and it names a second concierge or an expanded crew **only when the estimate actually staffs one** — the Concierges control set to 2, or more specialists than the app recommended. Corrected 2026-09-29: every rush line used to promise “a second Transition Concierge and an expanded specialist crew,” and the premium adds nobody, on a document that is Exhibit A of the signed agreement. It is charged on the services **before** any Preferred Client Discount, and the discount then comes off the labour *and the premium charged on it* — corrected 2026-09-23: this sentence described the order before 2026-09-11, which charged the premium on the discounted figure and let a discount quietly shrink it. Vendor costs are excluded (pass-through at cost doesn't get more expensive because we moved faster). Works on both hourly and fixed-price quotes, and not on standalone Home Prep, whose Schedule & Crew card is hidden.
  > **⚠ Two changes, 2026-09-30 (Anthony, Q9).** The premium is **never charged on the 30% home prep fee**, on either basis (*rush speeds up our crew, not the painter*): it is 20% of the services less that fee, and where a prep fee is on the estimate the premium's row says so. And **on a fixed price the premium is its own line under the fee**, 20% of whatever fee is quoted, so ticking rush always raises the price, even over a fee you typed. Until today it was folded into the suggested fee, shown on no fixed-price document, and ignored by a typed fee. Since the same day, a change order raised on an expedited fixed-price estimate saved under this rule is priced with the premium too (§9).
- **Quote as a fixed price** — the living-client services open on **time-and-materials** and the estates on a fixed price (2026-09-30, below); either way the concierge may switch an individual estimate. The amount prefills from the suggested fee — the labour as the job would run with two specialists (Crew, above) plus the materials package, with the service's contingency on top (**20%**; Probate 25%, Contested Probate 35%), and the 30% prep fee, the premium and the discount outside it — and is **your figure**: type the number the client will see, or tap *Round to $X* under the field for the suggestion rounded down to a clean figure. **Available on every service in the catalogue** as of 2026-09-20, Probate and Contested Probate included — until then the toggle was disabled on those two. The only engagement without it is **Home Prep for Sale**, where the box is hidden (2026-09-30), and that is not a policy exclusion: prep is billed as 30% of what the vendors actually invoice, plus any declutter hours as they are worked (§6a-i), so there is no labour price for a flat fee to replace. *Corrected 2026-09-30:* this bullet said every service bills time-and-materials by default, that the suggestion was the hourly basis plus a flat 20%, and that prep books no hours at all.
  > **⚠⚠ The fee is the price of the scope, and estates open on it (2026-09-30, Anthony on Q10 and Q13).** A new Estate Settlement, Probate or Contested Probate estimate opens with this box ticked; the living-client services open hourly; either way the box is yours. The suggested fee is the services and the contingency **with no premium and no discount inside it**: both print as their own lines under the fee on the estimate, the agreement and the invoices (see Rush order and Preferred Client Discount). **An estimate saved before today** carried both inside its fee; reopening it on Build Estimate restates the fee so the two can print as lines, the client's total stays where it was, and the fixed-price panel says what it did. Standalone Home Prep no longer shows this box at all (it was ignored there).
  > **The prefilled fee tracks the estimate until you type one.** It used to prefill once and then freeze while crew size, room scores, adjustments, the premium toggle, the discount and rush all kept moving the basis underneath it — a real estimate showed **$109,613 against a $77,620 services total**, a 41% markup where the contingency is 20%, because the toggle had been flipped when the total was around $91,300 and the crew changed afterwards. That figure goes on the client estimate, the agreement and every invoice, so a stale prefill is money. Type a number and it stops tracking — that is your price. Reopening a saved estimate always holds the client's total: a fee saved before 2026-09-23 or 2026-09-30 is restated so the prep fee, the premium and the discount print as lines, and the panel says what moved (the Q10 note above, the prep-fee note below).
  > **⚠ Rounding the fee is not an error, and the warning beside it now says only that the estimate moved (2026-09-20).** Anthony: *"if it's $25,275, I'd probably just create a $25,000 estimate."* The field is a money box now (it read `25275`, a bare number, before), and two chips sit under it: **Round to $X** — the suggestion rounded *down* to the nearest $1,000 above $20,000, $500 above $5,000, $100 below, so $25,275 becomes $25,000 — and **Suggested $Y**, which hands the field back to the tracker. Whatever you type is what prints on the client estimate, the agreement and all three invoices; measured on the real form, a $23,000 fee bills $11,500 · $5,750 · $5,750. The amber warning fires only when **the estimate has moved since you set the fee** — a room re-scored, the crew changed — and names both figures (*the suggested fee was $21,600 and is now $23,940*) with *Use* to take the new suggestion and *Keep* to stand by yours. It used to fire on the rounding itself: a $21,000 fee against a $21,600 suggestion read *+17% over the hourly basis … the estimate has moved since you set it* when nothing had moved, with a button offering to put the contingency straight back. That was the *"premium stuck in"*. An estimate saved before today carries no record of the suggestion it was set against, so it never warns until the fee is next touched.
  > The contingency is **20% on the living-client services and on Estate Settlement** — Estate Settlement was 25% and came down to match, because 25% on top of an already-buffered hourly estimate reads as padding to a client comparing quotes. **Probate carries 25% and Contested Probate 35%**, and as of 2026-09-20 those are figures a client sees rather than an internal reference: until then those two were time-and-materials only, so the band only ever drove the *"what a flat quote would run"* line on this tab. They were kept wide rather than flattened to 20% deliberately — the buffer *is* the risk Havellin absorbs, and the change-order triggers the estate agreement already lists (beneficiary disputes, legal holds and work stoppages, ancillary probate in another state) are precisely what makes a matter run long. It is a starting figure in any case: type over it, or tap *Round to $X*.
  > **⚠⚠ The 30% home prep fee is NOT inside the fixed fee (changed 2026-09-23).** On a job carrying Home Prep vendors the flat fee covers everything else, and the prep fee sits **on top of it as its own line**, charged on what the prep vendors actually bill — exactly as it is on a time-and-materials job. So the suggested fee **excludes** it (the contingency is no longer charged on the 30%), and the panel says how much sits outside the figure. Until today the fee was folded into the flat fee, marked up by the contingency with the rest of the basis and never trued to the vendors' bills — while the estimate, both agreements and every invoice said it was a separate fee charged on actuals. Anthony, off the estimate that surfaced it: *"yes, fix both."* **An estimate saved before today keeps the fee inside its flat fee until it is reopened**: its agreement and invoices print no second line and bill nothing extra. Reopening it on Build Estimate moves the fee out of the flat fee and on top of it, so the client's total is unchanged, and the panel says so — re-send it if the client has seen the old split.
- **Preferred Client Discount** — percentage off Havellin labor fees only, **capped at 15%**. The same cap applies to *Offer discount* on the timeline's *Estimate sent to client* step (§9a) — in the band while that step is lit, then in the strip at the foot of the timeline — which is the route to use once the estimate has already gone out. *Corrected 2026-09-30:* this named the *Estimate approved* row. The pop-up takes a whole number from 0 to 15, and **0 removes the discount** — until 2026-09-29 a blank or a 0 was quietly turned into 1%, so a discount could never be taken off. **The button goes once the signing packet has been sent, and so does Edit estimate**: after that the client is signing that price, and a change is a change order (§8, §9).
  > **On a fixed price (2026-09-30, Q13)** the discount is **its own line**, no longer folded into the fee: the percentage of the fee less the moving-materials package inside it, grossed up by the premium where there is one, the same rule as hourly labour. *Offer discount* uses exactly that rule; until today it took the percentage off the whole fee, package included, and disagreed with Build Estimate by about $180 on a job with a $1,500 package.
- **Moving Materials Package** — None, or an Estate tier (Basic $500 · Standard $750 · Premium $1,500) or Home Editing / Transition tier (Basic $200 · Standard $350 · Premium $550)

  > **⚠ IT IS A FIXED PACKAGE PRICE, NOT COST-PLUS — and four client surfaces said otherwise until 2026-09-11.** Two of them were **contracts**. They read *“billed at cost plus a 25% materials handling fee, itemized separately on the invoice. Receipts available on request.”* **There is no cost input anywhere in the app, no markup arithmetic and no receipt store** — the price is the dropdown you picked, and the invoice prints one line for it. The promise of *receipts* is the half that mattered: on a probate matter the representative may well ask, and the firm could not produce any. Every surface now states the same sentence and names the package the estimate quoted. **Nothing about what the client pays changed** — there was never a cost basis to mark up, so describing what is actually billed was the only available fix. If the package price internally embeds a markup, that is Havellin’s business and does not belong in the client’s contract.

### 5d. Third-Party Vendors & Moving Materials

Add third-party vendors with estimated costs. Vendor costs are estimates only — actual invoices are billed directly to the client at cost. **Havellin adds no fee to them.**

> **No coordination fee on third-party vendors, as of 2026-08-02.** Vendors are billed to the client directly at cost and Havellin adds nothing on top.
